question_type: project_related, source_types: confluence, question: finance is seeing qtd blended /1m output tokens jump. how do we verify whether its a rate card change or a streaming output token undercount, and what is the backfill and escalation path?, expected_doc_ids: dsid_330be20dd4224d8dad24eb633121ba06, dsid_c70d63e14aa84195aec43820c018f8b9, dsid_f952b60df24d4736b831a643993aee67, dsid_858fd59a63d64f5e81a2b8482ba64dbf, gold_answer: to separate a real rate card step-change from a streaming output token undercount, do two quick validations:nn(1) denominator / token completeness (most common: streaming parity gaps). compute hourly completeness_ratio against the canonical baseline:n- baseline: metering.output_tokens_hourly_by_routen- aggregate: unit_econ.hourly_aggregate (or analytics_unit_econ.ue_hourly_fact totalled with no dimension filters)n- completeness_ratio econ_output_tokens / baseline_output_tokensninterpretation: if completeness_ratio is outside 0.995 to 1.005 its suspicious if it is outside 0.990 to 1.010 for 2 consecutive hours treat as a sev-worthy token undercount (and page), and if any single hour is below 0.970 treat as an immediate sev1. then confirm it is streaming-specific by comparing streaming vs non-streaming avg output_tokens per request in metering.request_usage_hourly (look for streaming avg collapsing or near-zero), and optionally spot-check sampled raw gateway logs vs metered tokens (analytics.gateway_request_log_sample) for large negative deltas. if this points to streaming, check for recent gateway/runtime changes touching streaming, tool calls, truncation, or delivery semantics.nn(2) numerator / cost inputs and rate card version (if tokens are complete). verify cost input freshness and gaps:n- finance.gpu_cost_inputs_hourly max(effective_hour_ts) and look for region/provider hours with 0 or null gpu_cost_usd.nthen verify the applied rate card:n- check finance.gpu_rate_card (or finance_rate_cards.gpu_hourly_cost) for rate_card_version and effective start/end dates, and confirm the qtd window crosses an effective_date boundary. if rate_card_version rolled at month/quarter boundary, a step-change in blended /1m output tokens can be expected and should be annotated rather than treated as a pipeline bug.nnbackfill: once the root cause is fixed (or late metering/cost inputs arrive), backfill the smallest impacted hour range at hourly granularity using the idempotent job, e.g.:nredwoodctl unit-econ backfill --start yyyy-mm-ddthh:00:00z --end yyyy-mm-ddthh:00:00z --granularity hour --throttle medium --reason rebuild after streaming metering fix / late cost inputsn(or ueas backfill/run rerun for the specific event_hour). after backfill, re-verify hourly output_tokens and blended_cost_usd in the hourly fact table, and re-run the daily reconciliation view for impacted days (analytics.unit_econ_reconciliation_daily / analytics_unit_econ.vw_ue_reconciliation_vs_billing_daily).nnescalation path: telemetry and analytics on-call (eng platform / observability) is first response. if streaming/token instrumentation is implicated, engage nadia rahman (token metering) plus the runtime/gateway point-of-contact and open an incident if completeness is outside thresholds. if aggregation/warehouse job lag, failures, or timeouts are implicated, engage mei lin (data eng) and sre (sean gallagher) for warehouse load/partition issues. if the discrepancy is primarily rate card interpretation, allocation methodology, reconciliation tolerance, idle/overhead treatment, or month-end/qtd reporting impact, loop in fpa (rishi malhotra) for sign-off for sev1 exec-reporting risk, include the cfo sponsor (laura bennett)., answer_facts: to distinguish a rate card change from a streaming output token undercount, first validate output token completeness by computing an hourly completeness_ratio between unit econ output tokens and a canonical baseline output token source., the baseline for output tokens is metering.output_tokens_hourly_by_route., the unit econ aggregate for output tokens can be taken from unit_econ.hourly_aggregate or from analytics_unit_econ.ue_hourly_fact totaled with no dimension filters., completeness_ratio is defined as econ_output_tokens divided by baseline_output_tokens., if completeness_ratio is outside 0.995 to 1.005, it is suspicious for token undercount., if completeness_ratio is outside 0.990 to 1.010 for 2 consecutive hours, it should be treated as a sev-worthy token undercount and paged., if any single hour has completeness_ratio below 0.970, it should be treated as an immediate sev1., to confirm the issue is streaming-specific, compare streaming versus non-streaming average output_tokens per request in metering.request_usage_hourly and look for streaming averages collapsing or near-zero., an optional spot-check is to compare sampled raw gateway logs to metered tokens using analytics.gateway_request_log_sample and look for large negative deltas., if tokens are complete, verify cost input freshness by checking finance.gpu_cost_inputs_hourly for max(effective_hour_ts) and for region or provider hours with 0 or null gpu_cost_usd., to verify a rate card step-change, check the applied rate card in finance.gpu_rate_card or finance_rate_cards.gpu_hourly_cost for rate_card_version and effective start and end dates., a qtd blended /1m output tokens step-change can be expected if the qtd window crosses a rate card effective_date boundary, such as a month or quarter boundary rate_card_version roll., backfill should be run after the root cause is fixed or late metering or cost inputs arrive, and should target the smallest impacted hour range at hourly granularity using an idempotent backfill job., an example backfill invocation is redwoodctl unit-econ backfill with start and end timestamps, granularity hour, throttle, and a reason string., after backfill, re-verify hourly output_tokens and blended_cost_usd in the hourly fact table and re-run a daily reconciliation view for impacted days., daily reconciliation can be checked via analytics.unit_econ_reconciliation_daily or analytics_unit_econ.vw_ue_reconciliation_vs_billing_daily., escalation first response is telemetry and analytics on-call (eng platform or observability)., if streaming or token instrumentation is implicated, engage the token metering owner and the runtime or gateway point of contact and open an incident when completeness is outside thresholds., if aggregation or warehouse job lag, failures, or timeouts are implicated, engage data engineering and sre for warehouse load or partition issues., if the normal terminal event and (2) there is no coincident upstream failure (no upstream 5xx/timeout/reset) in a short correlation window (default 2s). if there is an upstream/gateway failure signal streaming reliability, and for streaming availability/success the request is only good if the stream is established and ends cleanly (terminal event clean close).nfor sla availability (calendar month): an eligible streaming request is failed if it ends in a redwood-attributable stream error/disconnect (or 5xx) client aborts/customer timeouts and customer/network issues supported by edge disconnect as client_abort only if the gateway observed a client-initiated close or reset before the normal terminal event and there is no coincident upstream failure signal within a short correlation window (about 2 seconds by default)., if there is any upstream or gateway failure signal (such as upstream 5xx, timeout, reset, gateway idle_timeout, proxy_write_error, or router failover midstream), from streaming reliability and excluded from streaming and latency slis per the exclusion rules., for slo and error budget accounting, platform-caused disconnects count against streaming reliability., calendar month, an eligible streaming request is failed if it ends in a platform-attributable stream error or disconnect or a 5xx response., for sla availability, client aborts, customer timeouts, and customer or network issues supported by edge telemetry are excluded., if disconnect cause is ambiguous, default to counting it as platform-attributable for sla and slo accounting., in customer-facing do any of the python, typescript, or go sdks still violate it based on the parity matrix and recent support tickets?, expected_doc_ids: dsid_0a23e8e094d5445b8a1af748598300ca, dsid_bced6413bb6f414a882fb9cbaf8c7448, dsid_89d75953da0c4bc186eb95fe169de462, dsid_3eb3b5da0ee540839a98c48f54fd1d5f, dsid_81744edbad5543b98dd551f883ac6772, dsid_c8e1d531048848c58247e00983096499, dsid_30b76ac074c841ef9afcb34c178c40d7, dsid_64212a1689814ac19deff834ce203ebd, dsid_a4bf9cb21b004bc9b12b8905c66896a4, gold_answer: v1 standard: streaming must be bounded by an overall_timeout_ms hard cap for the full lifecycle (including retries/backoff), plus a stream_idle_timeout_ms that limits time with no bytes/events (default 30000ms, resets on each valid chunk/event). safety-first streaming retry: never auto-retry once any stream output has been delivered retry is only allowed if the failure happens before the first byte/event (treating a pre-body 429/5xx/transport failure like a normal request, including honoring retry-after), and explicit client cancellation is never retried. if a disconnect happens after partial output, the sdk must raise a typed streaming interruption error (retryablefalse) with request_id when known and best-effort bytes/events received.nnbased on the parity matrix recent tickets: python had a known violation where the streaming iterator ignored configured timeouts (sup-29410) but this was fixed in redwood-sdk-python 0.18.0 and python now passes the stalled-stream/streaming-timeout conformance coverage (older 0.17.x still violates). typescript still partially violates the v1 timeout model for streaming because idle timeout is not implemented, and it has had streaming edge-case drift (disconnect classification / post-content retry suppression) even though the specific pre-first-byte 429 case (retry respect retry-after) was recently fixed and now passes conformance the earlier non-streaming 400 retry bug was also fixed in ts 2.8.2. go is improved on v1 retry behavior (retry-after on 429 is fixed in v0.10.0 and the streaming 429 retry-after path was brought into conformance), and it is generally aligned on no retry after events, but it still partially violates streaming timeout enforcement because stream idle timeout is not consistent/fully implemented per the parity matrix., answer_facts: v1 streaming must be bounded by an overall_timeout_ms hard cap for the full lifecycle including retries and backoff., v1 streaming must enforce a stream_idle_timeout_ms that limits time with no bytes or events., the default stream_idle_timeout_ms is 30000ms and it resets on each valid chunk or event., v1 safety-first streaming retry forbids any automatic retry once any stream output has been delivered., a retry is allowed only if the failure happens before the first byte or event, including pre-body 429, 5xx, or transport failures, and must honor retry-after when present., explicit client cancellation must never be retried., if a disconnect happens after partial output, the sdk must raise a typed streaming interruption error with retryablefalse, include request_id when known, and include best-effort bytes or events received., python had a known violation where the streaming iterator ignored configured timeouts (sup-29410)., the python timeout violation was fixed in redwood-sdk-python 0.18.0, while older 0.17.x still violates the v1 standard., typescript still partially violates the v1 streaming timeout model because stream idle timeout is not implemented., typescript has had streaming edge-case drift around disconnect classification and suppressing retries after content., go is generally aligned with the rule of no retry after streaming events have been delivered., go still question_id: qst_0369, question_type: project_related, source_types: confluence, github, google_drive, jira, question: why were some hosted api overload errors missing retry-after, and what changes and alerts ensure it stays consistent across gateway, routing, and streaming paths going forward?, expected_doc_ids: dsid_e7d8c585e8594fe481975515246d04f2, dsid_a560a94faf9c40ec9782d125d94fc009, dsid_9762ad5c4f7b45e3af5dda76b48752e4, dsid_5776ac1f724b4f72a084d3e796f0af4a, dsid_6e790a04abe54ce9a92877679790f915, dsid_12c56622cee749969076c8dbac19da4d, gold_answer: they were missing because the gateway only set retry-after on overload responses it generated itself (admission control / breaker paths). when overload was triggered downstream (routing or serving runtime) those services returned the correct overload json (overload_error / stable error.code) but often did not set headers, and the gateway sometimes passed the response through without normalizing headers. a related edge case was streaming: once an sse response has started and headers are committed, the gateway cannot reliably add retry-after, so late overload termination could not fix the header.nnthe fix was to centralize overload response construction and header normalization in the gateway: for any response mapped/classified as a retryable overload or rate limit condition (per adr-014 codes like overload_shed / overload_circuit_open / overload_queue_full / overload_dependency_unavailable, and 429 codes like rate_limit_exceeded / concurrency_limit_exceeded), the gateway now sets retry-after (integer seconds) unless it is already present, while preserving the downstream error.code/message. correlation ids were also tightened so clients always get a stable request id (prefer inbound x-request-id if provided, otherwise generate one) and the gateway echoes it back (with x-redwood-correlation-id additive during the deprecation window). for streaming, the gateway ensures overload admission/rejection happens before initiating the stream so the http error includes retry-after if streaming is already underway, the contract is to provide retry_after_ms in the terminal sse error event instead of relying on headers.nngoing forward, consistency is guarded by explicit observability and canary gates: the gateway emits a retry-after-missing counter (api_gateway.overload.retry_after_missing, with route_group/region/status dimensions) and a presence-rate view, and sre alerting pages when retry_after_present_rate drops below 98 over 15 minutes for overload-classified responses (with absent-metric guards for regions not yet fully deployed). these checks are part of the rollout/rollback criteria so regressions in retry-after or correlation headers stop expansion and trigger rollback before they impact more tenants., answer_facts: retry-after was missing because the gateway only set it on overload responses generated by the gateway itself (admission control or breaker paths)., when overload was triggered downstream in routing or the serving runtime, those services often did not set retry-after, and the gateway sometimes passed the response through without normalizing headers., for streaming (sse), if the stream had already started and headers were committed, the gateway could not reliably add retry-after, so late overload termination could not fix the header., the fix centralized overload response construction and header normalization in the gateway so overload and rate limit responses get consistent headers., for any response classified as a retryable overload or rate limit condition, the gateway now sets retry-after as integer seconds unless it is already present, while preserving the downstream error code and message., for streaming, the gateway ensures overload admission or rejection happens before initiating the stream so http error responses include retry-after if streaming is already underway, the terminal sse error event includes retry_after_ms instead of relying on headers., the gateway emits a retry-after-missing counter metric named api_gateway.overload.retry_after_missing (with route_group, region, and status dimensions) and a presence-rate view for retry-after on overload-classified responses., sre alerting pages when the retry-after present rate drops below 98 over 15 minutes for overload-classified responses (with guards for regions not fully deployed)., these observability checks are part of rollout and rollback criteria so regressions in retry-after stop expansion and trigger rollback before affecting more tenants., question_id: qst_0370, question_type: project_related, source_types: confluence, jira, slack, question: why can the observed canary traffic percentage in a single region diverge from what the console rollout step shows, and what are the recommended oncall mitigations and ga preventative fixes?, expected_doc_ids: dsid_8c24fd15fe5b473eb71341751d292b6e, dsid_150b485e7fd54251b937ab3d08a850dc, dsid_8ae540be8e4c458ca15e122040ea2418, dsid_94f568e4161d4d78b0db60af2b611746, dsid_6a8476f19fee43b3af984d2bd790453f, gold_answer: observed canary percent in one region can diverge from the console step because console/orchestrator shows the intended step target, but split enforcement is done in the smart routing data plane in each region and is applied independently per region (no attempt to keep a single global percent). since cohorting is computed after region selection, any change in regional routing (failover, capacity shifts, lack of region affinity) changes which requests are eligible in that region and can make the regional share look different even when config is correct. in addition, what you measure may be effective serving rather than intended cohort: if the intended canary target is unavailable or errors, fallback can cause intended_cohortcanary but effective_target/effective_cohortbaseline (or vice versa), so charts that do not distinguish intended vs effective can mislead. practical incident causes called out in ga/beta experience include multi-region propagation lag, stale route/rollout config cached in a subset of router pods (often due to a config watch disconnect with no periodic resync, leaving some pods enforcing an older percent like 25 while console shows 10), and router version skew in a single region using a legacy cohorting path or different fallback precedence/hash seed (biased assignment when stable keys are missing). finally, missing or unstable cohort keys (no x-redwood-cohort-key / idempotency-key) can make splits noisy across retries/stream reconnects, especially at low traffic.nnoncall mitigations: if there is any safety risk (canary taking materially more traffic than intended, or regressions), pause or rollback the rollout (rollback forces 0 canary) and confirm the change is actually applied in the affected region. if the issue is isolated to one region and tooling/flags allow, temporarily set that regions canary_pct to 0 while keeping other regions at the intended step. validate telemetry tag coverage first (rollout_id, intended_cohort, effective_cohort, region) and use routing decision logs/debug headers to compare console_step_target_percent vs configured_canary_percent and to confirm cohort_key_source/fallback_reason. then check per-region config apply health (route config versions, apply errors, stale-cache/watch errors, last apply age) if you see divergence or stale pods, force a resync (rolling restart of router pods in the region and/or operator forcerepublish/reconcile) and re-check that all sampled pods now report the same configured percent. escalate to smart routing if config/watch/caching or version skew is suspected, and to telemetry if cohort tags are missing use the kill switch only if normal pause/rollback/override do not take effect quickly.nnga preventative fixes: add a router watch-health watchdog plus periodic full resync, and emit/alert on router_config_watch_connected and router_config_last_apply_age_seconds (and config divergence/apply failures) to prevent stale percent enforcement after watch drops. prevent region router version skew with deploy parity monitoring and a control-plane/orchestrator guard that blocks/halts rollouts unless all eligible regions meet a minimum router version (and align hash implementation with golden test vectors). add observed-vs-expected split mismatch dashboards/alerts per route, rollout_id, and region (and intended vs effective mismatch panels) so phantom canary/fallback and partial-apply issues are visible quickly. improve console to surface region overrides/kill switches and intended vs effective cohort information, and reinforce docs/wizard guidance to send a stable x-redwood-cohort-key (or idempotency-key for retries/stream reconnects) and use region affinity/pinning when strict consistency across requests is required., standard redwood json error envelope and x-redwood-request-id header. the response body is error: ... with required fields error.coderesidency_policy_violation, error.subcode (region_not_allowed primary_region_unavailable policy_misconfigured), error.message, error.request_id (matching the header), and error.help_url. error.details must include allowed_regions (comma-separated public region ids), candidate_region, and policy_scope (org project api_key) primary_region and policy_id are optional. streaming behavior is part of the contract: enforcement must happen before any streaming bytes are written, and if blocked the server returns the same 409 json error body (no http 200 with an sse error event, no mid-stream generic 500, no silent close).nnin the gateway this is enforced by centralizing residency-violation mapping and serialization (the public error contract) so both rest handlers and the sse streaming proxy use the same code path the streaming proxy must preserve an upstream 409 residency error instead of rewriting non-2xx into a generic 500, and must evaluate residency pre-stream so headers/status are committed as 409 before any sse output.nnin the python sdk this is enforced in http error mapping: when status409 and the parsed error.code indicates a residency policy violation, raise a dedicated residencypolicyviolationerror (subclass of apierror) and carry through at least code/subcode/message plus request_id and help_url (and any available details like allowed_regions, candidate_region, policy_scope, primary_region/policy_id). for streaming, the sdk only raises this when the initial streaming response is an error mid-stream transport failures remain unchanged., answer_facts: the canonical data residency (region pinning) violation contract is dashboards, and audit history?, expected_doc_ids: dsid_e5b1e464966a48bf9406e6e7197fee0d, dsid_b5a4ac6f13d545b6b085fb74e971ff27, dsid_a6efecc786c9436499eb000b8489ae52, dsid_ab988030fc0c4544846f0e1f667453a3, gold_answer: a blocked profile (quality regression) is enforced as a hard policy constraint in the control-plane/policy layer: the profile is deterministically not allowed for the specific (model_id, variant, profile_id and often profile_version) even if the route still references it and it would otherwise pass technical compatibility. resolution returns baseline with an explicit blocked-style signal (distinct from normal incompatibility) so it is observable and does not depend on runtime behavior. an incompatibility fallback is the normal safety path when a profile was requested but fails compatibility or operational prerequisites (e.g., gpu/cuda/kernel unsupported, kv cache incompatible, max context/request-shape caps exceeded, profile not published in region/tier, artifact fetch timeout/integrity failure). in that case the system falls back to baseline with a machine-readable incompatibility/operational reason code.nnto verify a source_types: confluence, question: what is redwood inferences end-to-end process for making a production change (definition, required approvals, pre-deploy verification, deploy execution, customer/internal communications, and rollback/post-change requirements) for the hosted api and console?, expected_doc_ids: dsid_319db001cae246bbbb16f441b64d9b82, dsid_eca097630e444b2d8e30da4b08fcbc4e, dsid_2ee4c1a35c5f4dba9b49efd3e97dd265, dsid_2db615f2f6f14cbfae67c62b3c976b27, dsid_914d75cb27ee4a06b3f69e445d715ab4, dsid_81a7e3e0204d4c6984e0a503d5423314, dsid_bf7e0df04c8748458b5515c864c48609, dsid_190d9732b59b411d8a6bcc59fd61b309, gold_answer: for the hosted api and console, a production change is any change in any environment serving external customer traffic (including canary/regional prod) that could affect confidentiality, integrity, availability, latency, cost, or correctness (e.g., prod deploys, routing/model-selection policies, auth/rbac/quotas/billing, schema migrations/backfills, capacity/failover, or observability changes that reduce visibility). the change owner must create a durable change record (ticket) with summary customer impact assessment, risk checklist, verification plan, explicit rollback plan, links to prs/builds/dashboards/runbooks/migration plan, and captured approvals, and schedule within the default weekday 09:00-16:00 local window while obeying error-budget/incident change control (green: normal approvals yellow: add sre on-call red: freeze except incident/security fixes requiring ic service owner sre on-call).nnrequired approvals depend on change type and risk: standard needs 1 peer normal (most deploys) needs peer service owner and additionally sre gate for capacity/slo-sensitive/traffic routing/failover changes and security review for auth/session/rbac/secrets/encryption/audit logging/pii/retention/network exposure or security-relevant dependency upgrades (with threat summary, affected assets, security verification, safe rollback, and reviewer approval recorded) emergency changes require ic service owner (as available) and a completed change record/retrospective within 24 hours.nnpre-deploy verification (gates) for every release (unless an approved, documented exemption): provide build identifiers (image digest/build ), release-notes snippet, change record link, pr/test links ensure green unit integration tests (exceptions approved by service owner), perf sanity when request-path/caching/batching changes, confirm current slo posture (p95 latency/error rate normal), capacity headroom 20 or an sre-approved plan when changing scaling/concurrency/batching, ensure backward-compatible migrations with rollback/forward plan, and complete any required security gate checks.nndeploy execution: always follow the relevant runbook and announce internally in eng-releases (change record, owner/approvers, services/regions, canary plan, rollback trigger). for hosted api, perform pre-deploy checks (dashboards, no conflicting incidents, error-budget posture), deploy a mandatory canary (typically 1 region or 1 traffic) and hold 15 minutes steady-state pass criteria include 0.2 absolute error-rate regression, 10 p95 latency regression, and no sustained saturation (gpu/cpu/memory/queue depth), plus smoke tests (e.g., /v1/chat/completions streaming non-streaming, /v1/embeddings) and metering validation if billing changed then roll out region-by-region (default order us-west-2 canary us-east-1 eu-central-1) avoiding concurrent multi-region/routergateway rollouts unless explicitly approved. for the console, deploy console-api first, then console-web, prefer feature-flag dark launches and (when feasible) canary to an internal staff cohort validate key workflows (login, org switching, api key creation, usage dashboard, settings), flag evaluation health, and watch for auth errors (401/403) publish the new web bundle with cache invalidation while keeping the prior bundle available for fast revert.nncustomer and internal communications: internal heads-up in eng-releases is always required customer comms (status page notice and, for major impact, direct email) are required for expected downtime/degradation, breaking api/sdk/auth/quota changes, billing/usage reporting risk, or maintenance reducing redundancy-using 24h lead for minor and 72h email for major maintenance (emergency: asap), including what/impact/times/regions/products/mitigations/status-page link/support contact, with updates at least hourly during the window and a final completion update.nnrollback and post-change requirements: stop the line and rollback immediately if canary/post-deploy gates fail or if theres plausible sev2 impact (including the explicit triggers: 0.2 sustained error-rate increase, 10 sustained p95 latency increase, saturation/timeouts, or customer reports) rollback by pausing further rollout, reverting highest-leverage toggles first (feature flags, routing/policy bundles), then redeploying the last known-good image digest/bundle (hosted api typically region-by-region starting with the most impacted), with special handling/escalation for schema migrations and validation of positive/negative authz for auth/rbac changes. after any change (successful or rolled back), perform functional smoke tests, verify observability (dashboards/logs/alerts), validate billing integrity if relevant, and keep enhanced monitoring for 60 minutes only close the change record once it documents final build state (or rollback), evidence/links of observed outcomes, timestamps, and any follow-up tickets., answer_facts: for hosted api and console, a production change is any change in any environment serving external customer traffic, including canary or regional prod., a production change includes changes that could affect confidentiality, integrity, availability, latency, cost, or correctness., the change owner must create a durable change record (ticket) for the production change., the change record must include a summary, customer impact assessment, risk checklist, verification plan, explicit rollback plan, links to relevant prs/builds/dashboards/runbooks/migration plan, and captured approvals., production changes should be scheduled within the default weekday 09:00 to 16:00 local window., production changes must obey error-budget or incident change control: green allows normal approvals, yellow adds sre on-call involvement, and red freezes changes except incident or security fixes requiring ic plus service owner plus sre on-call., standard changes require approval from one peer., normal changes require approval from a peer and the service owner., normal changes also require an sre gate for capacity, slo-sensitive, traffic routing, or failover changes., normal changes require security review for auth, session, rbac, secrets, encryption, audit logging, pii, retention, network exposure, or security-relevant dependency upgrades, with the review recorded., emergency changes require ic approval and service owner involvement as available, and a completed change record or retrospective within 24 hours., pre-deploy verification is required for every release unless there is an approved, documented exemption., pre-deploy verification includes providing build identifiers and links to release notes, change record, and prs or tests., pre-deploy verification requires green unit and integration tests unless exceptions are approved by the service owner., pre-deploy verification requires a performance sanity check when request-path, caching, or batching changes are made., pre-deploy verification includes confirming current slo posture such as p95 latency and error rate being normal., pre-deploy verification requires at least 20 percent capacity headroom or an sre-approved plan when changing scaling, concurrency, or batching., pre-deploy verification requires backward-compatible migrations with rollback and forward plans for schema changes., pre-deploy verification requires completing any required security gate checks., deploy execution must follow the relevant runbook., deploy execution requires an internal announcement in eng-releases including change record, owner and approvers, services and regions, canary plan, and rollback trigger., for hosted api deploys, pre-deploy checks include dashboards review, confirming no conflicting incidents, and checking error-budget posture., for hosted api deploys, a mandatory canary is required, typically one region or 1 percent traffic, held for at least 15 minutes at steady state., hosted api canary pass criteria include no more than 0.2 percent absolute error-rate regression, no more than 10 percent p95 latency regression, and no sustained resource saturation., hosted api deploy verification includes smoke tests and metering validation when billing is changed., after hosted api canary success, rollout proceeds region-by-region in the default order us-west-2 canary, then us-east-1, then eu-central-1., hosted api deploys should avoid concurrent multi-region or router-plus-gateway rollouts unless explicitly approved., for console deploys, console-api is deployed first and then console-web., console deploys should prefer feature-flag dark launches and, when feasible, canary to an internal staff cohort., console deploy verification includes validating key workflows such as login, org switching, api key creation, usage dashboard, and settings, and watching for auth errors like 401 or 403., console deploys publish the new web bundle with cache invalidation while keeping the prior bundle available for fast revert., internal communication in eng-releases is always required for production changes., customer communications (status page notice and for major impact direct email) are required for expected downtime or degradation, breaking api or sdk or auth or quota changes, billing or usage reporting risk, or maintenance that reduces redundancy., customer communications should provide at least 24 hours lead time for minor maintenance and at least 72 hours lead time plus email for major maintenance, with emergency communications sent as soon as possible., customer communications should include what is changing, impact, timing, regions, products, mitigations, a status-page link, and a support contact., during the maintenance window, customer communications should be updated at least hourly and include a final completion update., rollback is required immediately if canary or post-deploy gates fail or if there is plausible sev2 or higher impact., explicit rollback triggers include sustained error-rate increase greater than 0.2 percent, sustained p95 latency increase greater than 10 percent, saturation or timeouts, or customer reports., rollback execution includes pausing further rollout, reverting highest-leverage toggles first such as feature flags or routing or policy bundles, and redeploying the last known-good build., hosted api rollback typically proceeds region-by-region starting with the most impacted region., rollback requires special handling and escalation for schema migrations and validating authorization behavior for auth or rbac changes., after any change, successful or rolled back, functional smoke tests must be performed., after any change, observability must be verified, including dashboards, logs, and alerts., after any change, billing integrity must be validated when relevant., after any change, enhanced monitoring must be maintained for at least 60 minutes., the change record can be closed only after documenting the final build state or rollback, evidence and links of outcomes, timestamps, and any follow-up tickets., question_id: qst_0443, question_type: completeness, source_types: fireflies, gmail, slack, question: list every internal communication thread (email, slack, and question_type: completeness, source_types: confluence, question: what is redwood inferences complete end-to-end process for shipping an emergency serving-runtime hotfix to production (hosted dedicated), including required approvals, checklists, rollout/rollback steps, and mandatory customer communications?, expected_doc_ids: dsid_452dc45d76754cbca8a3674170599633, dsid_e5fe3fd0cf444668a64970ec00818f46, dsid_ac2fdba09cc24cd98ba60f5da114780c, dsid_8c3d5e26a1c841199d3bcd7aea5d495e, dsid_6807c651b55546559733c7c6f8dc133d, dsid_5d509e588f994166a2ccc6e39cbf6cb5, gold_answer: to ship an emergency serving-runtime hotfix to production (hosted dedicated), redwood inference follows this end-to-end process:nn1) open the emergency change record the minimum checklist (in a linear/jira ticket and the incident timeline/thread): state the fix hypothesis, whats changing (code/config/infra), blast radius (hosted regions, dedicated clusters/customers, model families), risk assessment (security/data/perf), a rollback plan with explicit triggers, and who owns post-change monitoring ensure an incident channel/thread exists with an identified ic.nn2) get required approvals (no exceptions): two-person approval from (a) ic (or sre on-call) and (b) the serving-runtime service owner (or delegate), recorded in pr review/incident thread/change record. add scenario-based approvals as applicable: security on-call (security/exploit), product on-call/pm (customer-visible behavior changes), and customer success escalation manager (csem) when dedicated/customer maintenance coordination is required.nn3) prepare the hotfix pr (serving runtime hotfix runbook): cut hotfix/runtime--, cherry-pick only the minimal fix, update the runtime hotfixes changelog, open a pr (prefer release branch), and obtain emergency reviews from runtime on-call sre on-call (or ic) include incident link, risk, rollback, and rollout owners.nn4) build supply-chain sign artifacts (mandatory): run the approved hotfix build pipeline to produce the new runtime image in the production registry plus sbom and provenance attestation, and verify cosign signatures/attestations before rollout image signing cannot be skipped (sbom delay is only allowed with security on-call approval and must be attached within 4 hours).nn5) validate before rollout (minimum required even in sev-1): run unit tests for the affected module, one perf canary for a representative model, and a stress test matching the incident pattern record results in the pr confirming error-rate improvement, no 10 p95 regression (same workload), and no increase in oom/gpu reset counters.nn6) post rollout notes to the incident channel: new runtime image tag, expected rollout impact (restarts/brief resets), the canary target, and rollback criteria (metrics customer symptom). a canary is required unless explicitly waived by ic (with rationale recorded).nn7) roll out to hosted (platform/sre hosted rollout rollback procedure): announce rollout start tag set runtime_image_tag and sync via argocd (hosted-runtime). roll out in order us-west-2a canary (10) us-west-2 (50) us-east-1 (50) eu-central-1 (50) global, monitoring each step for at least 15 minutes (success rate, p95 for top routes, gpu oom, router fallback, error-budget burn). rollback immediately by reverting to the last-known-good tag and argocd syncing if 5xx/stream aborts persist 5 min, oom spikes 2 baseline, p95 regresses 20 for 10 min, crash loops appear, or customer reports correlate with rollout start announce rollback and next steps in the incident channel.nn8) roll out to dedicated (infra csem dedicated cluster hotfix rollout notes): collect required inputs (customer/cluster id, current/target tags, expected restart impact, rollback tag, scope), obtain approvals (ic/sre dedicated service owner csem sign-off on messaging), and communicate to customers (unless ic explicitly waives due to active outage): state brief interruption risk, provide a rollout window, and describe rollback criteria in plain language. canary one dedicated cluster first (prefer internal dogfood/lowest-traffic with csem approval) and validate 30 minutes (success rate, p95, oom, restart frequency, customer health checks) then schedule remaining clusters, rolling no more than 3 in parallel unless ic approves. per cluster: confirm comms sent, update helm runtime_image_tag, argocd sync dedicated--runtime, watch for crashloopbackoff, run customer smoke checks (chat/embeddings/streaming). if rollback triggers hit, revert the tag, restart if needed, and notify the customer immediately (csem).nn9) mandatory sev-1 customer communications (status page customer notifications): ic owns accuracy/cadence csem owns direct enterprise/dedicated outreach initial acknowledgment within 10 minutes, updates every 30 minutes while impact continues, and a resolution message within 15 minutes of confirmed mitigation. each update must include customer symptoms, affected surfaces (regions/clusters/models), mitigation steps, eta with confidence level, and any workarounds provide a post-incident summary within 1 business day (required for enterprise).nn10) post-change closeout: update the incident timeline with start/change/outcome, record cluster/region outcomes, and within 2 business days complete incident follow-up (what changed and why it worked), document any release-process deviations/corrective actions, remove temporary flags/throttles (with owner deadline), and create a task to promote the hotfix into the next regular release., answer_facts: an emergency serving-runtime hotfix requires opening an emergency change ticket and recording a minimum checklist: fix hypothesis, what is changing (code/config/infra), blast radius (hosted regions and dedicated clusters/customers), risk assessment (security/data/perf), an explicit rollback plan with triggers, and a named owner for post-change monitoring, with an incident channel/thread and identified incident commander (ic)., required approvals for an emergency serving-runtime hotfix are two-person approval from (a) the ic or sre on-call and (b) the serving-runtime service owner (or delegate), recorded in the pr review or incident thread/change record., additional scenario-based approvals apply: security on-call for security or exploit risk, product on-call or pm for customer-visible behavior changes, and a customer success escalation manager (csem) when dedicated or customer maintenance coordination is required., the hotfix pr process includes cutting a hotfix branch, cherry-picking only the minimal fix, updating the runtime hotfixes changelog, and opening a pr (prefer the release branch) that includes incident link, risk, rollback plan, and rollout owners, with emergency reviews from runtime on-call and sre on-call (or ic)., before rollout, the hotfix build must produce a new runtime image in the production registry along with sbom and provenance attestation, and cosign signatures/attestations must be verified image signing cannot be skipped, and any sbom delay requires security on-call approval and must be attached within 4 hours., minimum pre-rollout validation is required even in sev-1: unit tests for the affected module, a performance canary for a representative model, and a stress test matching the incident pattern, with results recorded (including error-rate improvement, no more than 10 percent p95 regression on the same workload, and no increase in oom/gpu reset counters)., before executing rollout steps, the incident channel must be updated with the new runtime image tag, expected rollout impact (restarts/brief resets), the canary target, and rollback criteria a canary is required unless explicitly waived by the ic with rationale recorded., hosted rollout is executed by platform/sre by setting runtime_image_tag to the new tag and syncing via argocd for hosted-runtime, with rollout in order: us-west-2a canary (10 percent), then us-west-2 (50 percent), us-east-1 (50 percent), eu-central-1 (50 percent), then global., hosted rollout requires monitoring each step for at least 15 minutes using metrics including success rate, p95 for top routes, gpu oom, router fallback, and error-budget burn., hosted rollback is performed by reverting to the last-known-good tag and argocd syncing if any rollback trigger occurs, and the rollback plus next steps must be announced in the incident channel., hosted rollback triggers include: 5xx or stream aborts persisting more than 5 minutes, oom spikes more than 2x baseline, p95 latency regressing more than 20 percent for more than 10 minutes, crash loops, or customer reports correlating with rollout start., dedicated rollout is executed by infra with csem involvement, after collecting required inputs (customer/cluster id, current/target tags, expected restart impact, rollback tag, and scope) and obtaining approvals from ic/sre, the dedicated service owner, and csem sign-off on customer messaging., for dedicated, customers must be communicated to (unless the ic explicitly waives due to active outage), including brief interruption risk, a rollout window, and rollback criteria in plain language., dedicated rollout must canary one cluster first (prefer internal dogfood or lowest-traffic with csem approval) and validate for about 30 minutes using metrics including success rate, p95, oom, restart frequency, and customer health checks., after dedicated canary validation, remaining clusters are scheduled with no more than 3 clusters in parallel unless the ic approves more., per dedicated cluster, execution includes confirming customer communications were sent, updating helm runtime_image_tag, argocd syncing dedicated--runtime, monitoring for crashloopbackoff, and running customer smoke checks (chat/embeddings/streaming)., if dedicated rollback triggers are hit, the tag is reverted (and restarted if needed) and the customer must be notified immediately by csem., mandatory sev-1 customer communications include use of a status page and customer notifications, with the ic owning accuracy and cadence and the csem owning direct enterprise/dedicated outreach., sev-1 communications timing requirements are: initial acknowledgment within 10 minutes, updates every 30 minutes while impact continues, and a resolution message within 15 minutes of confirmed mitigation., each sev-1 customer update must include customer symptoms, affected surfaces (regions/clusters/models), mitigation steps, eta with confidence level, and any workarounds., a post-incident summary is required within 1 business day for enterprise customers., post-change closeout includes updating the incident timeline with start/change/outcome and recording region/cluster outcomes, completing incident follow-up within 2 business days (what changed and why it worked), documenting release-process deviations and corrective actions, removing temporary flags/throttles with an owner and deadline, and creating a task to promote the hotfix into the next regular release.,
